# Luma Icon System

### Canon Draft v1

Goal:

Icons must be:

• instantly recognizable  
• readable at small size  
• consistent in weight and geometry  
• visible even with reduced vision  
• language-independent

---

# 1. Icon Shape Language

All icons follow the same structural grammar.

### Stroke style

```
2.5px stroke
rounded line caps
rounded joins
```

Reason:

Sharp corners reduce readability at small sizes.

---

### Fill

Icons are **outline-only**.

No fills except when representing **active state**.

This prevents visual heaviness.

---

### Grid

Icons must fit a strict grid.

```
24 × 24 grid
```

Stroke alignment:

```
centered stroke
```

---

### Corner radius rule

Any interior angle must follow:

```
minimum radius: 2px
```

This prevents harsh geometry.

---

# 2. Icon Size

We define three canonical sizes.

### Quadrant icon

```
28 px
```

This is the main interaction size.

---

### Secondary UI icons

```
20 px
```

Used for lists or settings.

---

### Micro icons

```
16 px
```

Used in metadata contexts.

---

# 3. Visual Contrast

Icons must pass a **low-vision visibility test**.

Minimum contrast ratio:

```
4.5:1
```

Default color:

```
soft white on charcoal
```

State colors:

```
teal
amber
violet
```

Glow is applied to the **shield**, not the icon.

---

# 4. Texture Rules

Icons must appear **clean and matte**.

Forbidden:

```
gradients
3D effects
glass reflections
emboss
drop shadows
```

Allowed:

```
soft glow when active
```

But only minimal.

---

# 5. Icon Recognition Rule

Every icon must pass the **3-meter test**.

If the user can recognize the icon at a glance from:

```
3 meters
```

on a phone screen, it passes.

If not, it fails.

---

# 6. Canon Icon Inventory

We should lock the core set used in the shield.

Proposed base set:

### Group

Icon concept:

```
two overlapping circles
```

Meaning:

```
people / contacts / circle
```

---

### Nudge

Icon concept:

```
pulse / signal wave
```

Meaning:

```
ping / check-in
```

---

### Alternate Nudge

Icon concept:

```
double pulse
```

Meaning:

```
stronger or different nudge
```

---

### Discovery

Icon concept:

```
compass / navigation marker
```

Meaning:

```
explore shields
```

---

### Shield

Icon concept:

```
Luma shield outline
```

Meaning:

```
core action / protection
```

---

### Hive / Group Context

Icon concept:

```
hex cluster
```

Meaning:

```
group shield
```

---

# 7. Icon Alignment Rules

Icons must always sit:

```
centered in quadrant
```

with:

```
minimum padding: 10px
```

This prevents touch ambiguity.

---

# 8. Accessibility Rule

Icons must remain distinguishable for:

• color blindness  
• reduced vision  
• glare environments

Therefore icons cannot rely on:

```
color differences alone
```

Shape must carry meaning.

---

# 9. Interaction Feedback

When tapped, icon behavior:

```
slight scale (1.05)
micro glow
```

Duration:

```
120 ms
```

This gives tactile confirmation.

---

# 10. Icon Design Philosophy

The icons should feel like:

```
tools
not decorations
```

Minimal, functional, timeless.

If someone looks at the UI in ten years, the icons should still feel modern.

---

Before generating screens again, we must **audit our icon inventory**.

# Luma Icon System

## Canon Registry v1

This registry defines the **official interaction icon set** used across all shields and screens.

Icons represent **verbs**, not nouns.

Meaning is conveyed by:

```
shape
motion
context
```

not text.

---

# 1. Icon Geometry Standard

All icons follow identical geometry.

### Grid

```
24 × 24 unit grid
```

All icons must fit this grid.

---

### Stroke

```
2.5px stroke
rounded caps
rounded joins
```

This improves readability on OLED displays.

---

### Padding

Icons must maintain:

```
3 unit minimum padding
```

within the grid.

---

### Corner radius rule

Minimum interior curve:

```
2 units
```

No sharp angles.

---

# 2. Icon Size System

Three sizes exist.

### Shield quadrant icons

```
28px
```

Primary interaction size.

---

### Secondary UI icons

```
20px
```

Used in lists, panels.

---

### Micro icons

```
16px
```

Metadata contexts.

---

# 3. Texture Rules

Icons must remain visually neutral.

Forbidden:

```
gradients
3D shading
glass effects
drop shadows
```

Allowed:

```
soft glow when active
```

Icons themselves remain matte.

---

# 4. Color Rules

Default icon color:

```
#F2F4F6
```

States:

```
teal   → active
amber  → attention
violet → urgent
```

Icons never use red or green.

---

# 5. Canon Icon Inventory (Core)

These icons must exist in the system.

---

# Luma Shield Quadrant Layout Blueprint

### Canon v1

This blueprint defines the **exact spatial relationships** between the shield, quadrants, and icons.

All measurements scale relative to the shield diameter.

Let:

```
S = shield diameter
```

---

# 1. Shield Diameter

The shield occupies:

```
0.68 × screen width
```

This places it inside the **natural thumb interaction zone**.

---

# 2. Center Shield Anchor

The center shield icon is:

```
0.26 × S
```

Purpose:

• visual anchor  
• primary action  
• interaction focus

---

# 3. Shield Gap Ring

Between the center shield and quadrants there must be a gap.

```
0.03 × S
```

Purpose:

• visual breathing room  
• prevents quadrant collision  
• improves touch clarity

---

# 4. Quadrant Interaction Band

Quadrants exist inside a ring.

Inner radius:

```
(C/2) + gap
```

Outer radius:

```
0.50 × S
```

This band aligns with **natural thumb arcs**.

---

# 5. Quadrant Shape

Quadrants are **curved chambers**, not pie slices.

Forbidden:

```
radial cross lines
```

Required shape:

```
four curved chambers
```

Visually they resemble:

```
petals around a core
```

This creates the **shield aesthetic**.

---

# 6. Icon Placement

Icons sit on a radial line.

Distance from center:

```
0.34 × S
```

This is the optical center of each quadrant.

---

# 7. Icon Size

Quadrant icons are:

```
28 px
```

On high-resolution devices this maintains clarity.

---

# 8. Quadrant Padding

Icons must maintain minimum padding from chamber edge:

```
10 px
```

This prevents touch ambiguity.

---

# 9. Quadrant Orientation

Standard orientation:

```
Q1 top-left
Q2 top-right
Q3 bottom-right
Q4 bottom-left
```

This remains constant across all shields.

Meaning is determined by **icon and context**, not location.

---

# 10. Touch Area

Each quadrant must support a touch radius of:

```
44 px minimum
```

This follows mobile accessibility standards.

---

# Result

The shield becomes:

```
center anchor
four curved chambers
icons positioned on radial lines
```

This structure ensures:

• clarity  
• visual harmony  
• ergonomic use

# Canon Addition

## Icon / Text Comfort Layer

### Principle

Luma remains **icon-native**, but users may optionally enable **text reinforcement**.

Icons remain the **primary interaction language**.

Text acts as **interpretive assistance**.

---

# Behavior Model

Default mode:

```
icon only
```

Optional mode:

```
icon + text label
```

Text appears **beneath the icon inside the quadrant chamber**.

---

# Toggle Mechanism

The toggle must be **one tap** and extremely discoverable.

Recommended placement:

```
top-right micro control
```

Icon:

```
Aa
```

Behavior:

Tap toggles between:

```
Icon Mode
Icon + Text Mode
```

---

# Animation Behavior

When switching modes:

```
icons remain fixed
text fades in below icon
```

Animation timing:

```
150 ms fade
```

No layout shift of the shield.

---

# Text Rendering Rules

Text size:

```
12 px
```

Font weight:

```
medium
```

Alignment:

```
centered under icon
```

Maximum characters:

```
12
```

Overflow handling:

```
ellipsis
```

---

```
   👥
```

```
   👥
  Circle
```

It improves accessibility without degrading the icon system.

or

Icon
Text

All three represent the **same behavior layer**.

Only the **visual representation changes**.

# Canon Principle

```
Interaction meaning must not depend on representation.
Representation must adapt to the individual.
```

The system remains consistent regardless of mode.

# Mode Definitions

### Icon Mode

Purpose:

Fastest visual comprehension.

Best for users who think visually.

Quadrant displays:

```
icon only
```

Example:

```
👥
```

---

### Text Mode

Purpose:

Maximum clarity.

Best for users who prefer words or have difficulty interpreting symbols.

Quadrant displays:

```
text only
```

Example:

```
Circle
```

---

### Icon + Text Mode

Purpose:

Learning bridge.

Best for onboarding or mixed cognition.

Quadrant displays:

```
icon
text beneath
```

Example:

```
👥
Circle
```

---

# Switching Modes

Switch must be:

• visible  
• simple  
• reversible

Recommended control:

```
top corner control
```

Icon:

```
A
```

Behavior:

Tap cycles through modes.

```
Icon → Text → Icon + Text → Icon
```

---

# Animation Behavior

Mode changes must feel smooth.

Transition rules:

```
icons remain anchored
text fades in or out
```

Duration:

```
150 ms
```

No shield movement.

### Icon Mode

Icon centered in quadrant.

```
28 px
```

---

### Text Mode

Text centered.

```
14 px font
```

---

Icon shifts slightly upward.

icon offset 4 px
```

Text appears beneath.

# 1. Quadrant Verb Dictionary

### Canon v1

Quadrants represent **verbs**, not features.

Each verb corresponds to **one icon and one behavior**.

| Verb | Icon Concept | Behavior |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Connect | Two overlapping circles | Establish trusted link |
| Nudge | Pulse wave | Send lightweight check-in |
| Discover | Compass | Explore shields or contexts |
| Share | Outgoing arrow | Share shield or state |
| Respond | Reply arrow | Respond to incoming interaction |
| Navigate | Direction arrow | Navigate to shield context |
| Assist | Helping hand | Offer assistance |
| Confirm | Checkmark | Confirm interaction |
| Record | Circle capture | Save moment/state |
| Alert | Soft beacon | Attention request |
| Locate | Target marker | Find context |
| Status | Dot indicator | Display presence |
| Invite | Shield outline | Invite trusted connection |
| Group | Hex cluster | Open group shield |
| Close | X mark | Exit context |
| Archive | Folder | Store shield |
| Protect | Shield icon | Activate protection behavior |

This dictionary prevents **verb ambiguity**.

---

# 2. Icon Reference Chart

All icons follow the same visual grammar.

### Geometry

```
24 × 24 grid
2.5 px stroke
rounded caps
rounded joins
```

### Style

```
outline only
no gradients
no shadows
no 3D
```

### Contrast

```
minimum 4.5:1
```

### State colors

```
neutral white
teal active
amber attention
violet urgent
```

Icons remain **matte**.

Glow belongs to **shield interactions**, not the icon.

---

# 3. Shield Geometry Diagram

### Canon Blueprint

Define the mathematical shield layout.

Let:

```
S = shield diameter
```

---

### Shield size

```
S = 0.68 × screen width
```

---

### Center shield anchor

```
0.26 × S
```

---

### Interaction ring

Quadrant band:

```
inner radius = center radius + gap
outer radius = 0.50 × S
```

Gap:

```
0.03 × S
```

---

### Icon placement

Radial center distance:

```
0.34 × S
```

Icon size:

```
28 px
```

Padding:

```
10 px minimum
```

---

### Quadrant orientation

```
Top-Left
Top-Right
Bottom-Right
Bottom-Left
```

Meaning is determined by **icon and context**, not location.

# 1. Icon Geometry System

### Grid

```
24 × 24 grid
```

Every icon must fit exactly inside this grid.

---

### Stroke

```
2.5 px stroke
rounded line caps
rounded joins
```

This improves OLED legibility and prevents harsh edges.

---

### Padding

Minimum internal padding:

```
3 units
```

Icons must not touch the grid boundary.

---

### Corner Radius

Minimum curve radius:

```
2 units
```

No sharp corners.

---

# 2. Icon Size System

Three canonical sizes exist.

### Shield Quadrant Icons

```
28 px
```

Primary interaction icons.

---

### Secondary Icons

```
20 px
```

Lists, menus, settings.

---

### Micro Icons

```
16 px
```

Metadata or compact contexts.

---

# 3. Icon Texture Rules

Icons must remain visually neutral.

Forbidden:

```
gradients
drop shadows
glass effects
3D rendering
embossing
```

Allowed:

```
outline icons only
```

Active state may include **soft glow**.

---

# 4. Color Rules

Default icon color:

```
soft white
```

Interaction states:

```
teal → active
amber → attention
violet → urgent
```

Icons must remain recognizable **without color**.

# Verdict

The generated icons are **not built to the specs we set**, and they also fail the **zero cognitive load / one-look understanding** requirement.

I should have caught that faster.

# Where they fail

## 1. Line-art only is too thin for primary shield use

For a general UI system, monoline outline icons can work.

For **Luma quadrant icons**, especially under:

* fast glance use
* imperfect vision
* stress
* glow environments
* low-light phone viewing

they are too fragile.

That creates:

* slower recognition
* more edge interpretation
* weaker silhouette memory

So your diagnosis is right: **higher cognitive load**.

## 2. They are visually inconsistent

The generated set mixes:

* outline thickness feel
* optical weight
* curvature logic
* fill behavior

That violates the consistency standard we wanted.

## 3. Some symbols are semantically weak

A few are too abstract to read in one glance.

If an icon needs interpretation, it fails Luma law.

## 4. They are not optimized for low-vision clarity

The shapes are not bold enough and the silhouettes are not strong enough.

That directly breaks your requirement:

> clean and understood in one look even with less than perfect vision

# Important correction to canon

We should update the icon canon now.

The earlier “outline-only / no fill” rule is **too rigid for Luma primary interaction icons**.

That was a mistake for this system.

## New icon law

For **primary shield quadrant icons**:

* **solid or mostly solid silhouette-first icons**
* minimal internal detail
* strong outer shape
* optional small cutouts only where needed
* no decorative line complexity

For **secondary UI icons**:

* monoline can still be used in settings, lists, metadata

So the correct split is:

## Primary icons

**Bold silhouette icons**

## Secondary icons

**Outline / monoline icons**

That is the right system.

# Why this is better

A bold silhouette:

* reads faster
* survives small sizes better
* works better under glow
* works better for aging eyes
* reduces interpretation time

That matches your law:  
**understood in one look**

# Updated canonical icon rule

## Shield quadrant icons

* 1-color solid silhouette
* high contrast
* simple massing
* no more than 1 interior cutout
* recognizable by silhouette alone

## Secondary/supporting icons

* monoline allowed
* lighter visual weight acceptable

# So, are the current icons usable?

**No.**  
Not as canonical quadrant icons.

They may be rough conceptual placeholders for discussion, but they are **not production canon**.

# What we should do next

We need to rebuild the icon system around **silhouette recognition**, not outline elegance.

That means defining:

1. **primary quadrant icon shape language**
2. **secondary icon shape language**
3. **icon optical weight rules**
4. **fill / cutout rules**
5. **recognition test**

# Recognition test we should lock

A primary icon passes only if:

* readable at 28 px
* recognizable in grayscale
* recognizable with blur
* recognizable in under 500 ms
* distinguishable from every other primary icon by silhouette alone

That is the correct bar.

# Bottom line

You were right to stop this.

The current set:

* does **not** meet the spec
* does **not** meet the law
* should **not** be treated as canon

# Next-step recommendations

1. Replace the old icon rule with **silhouette-first primary icons / monoline secondary icons**.
2. Define the **primary quadrant icon build spec**.
3. Rebuild the icon inventory under that spec.
4. Create a new **primary icon reference sheet**.
5. Then resume render plates.
6. **Execute All (Sequential)**

**ChatGPT:**

You’re right to challenge it. The sheet that was generated is **not acceptable for what you’re building**. And the deeper issue you pointed out is the real one:

> If the user needs a cheat sheet, the icon system has failed.

For a system like **Luma Shield**, where decisions may happen **quickly and under cognitive load**, relying on a custom icon language is risky. The best approach is not inventing new symbols. It is using **proven, globally learned iconography** and only designing new icons where absolutely unavoidable.

Let’s ground this in what research and large-scale products have already proven.

---

# The Best Proven Solution

The most reliable approach used by systems like **iOS, Android Material, aviation dashboards, and medical interfaces** is:

### Use **universally learned symbols first**, then add text reinforcement if needed.

People already know many icons because they have been trained by decades of interface exposure.

Examples that require **almost no interpretation**:

* **+** add / invite
* **✓** confirm
* **×** close / cancel
* **🔍** search / discover
* **📍** location
* **👥** people / group
* **→** navigation / go
* **🔔** alert
* **●** status / presence

Those symbols work because they rely on **pre-existing mental models**.

Trying to invent a new language of icons almost always increases cognitive load.

---

# The Real Rule for Luma

Instead of:

“Design beautiful icons”

The rule should be:

> Use **icons people already understand without thinking**.

If we do that, the interface becomes instantly readable.

---

# The Luma Icon Strategy (Recommended)

### Tier 1 — Universal Icons (Use directly)

These require almost zero learning.

| Function | Symbol |
| --- | --- |
| Group | 👥 |
| Confirm | ✓ |
| Close | × |
| Search / Discover | 🔍 |
| Location | 📍 |
| Status | ● |
| Share | ↗ |
| Navigate | → |

---

### Tier 2 — Modified Universal Icons

These are slightly adapted but still familiar.

| Function | Symbol Concept |
| --- | --- |
| Invite | + with person |
| Assist | hand |
| Alert | bell |
| Archive | box |

---

### Tier 3 — Luma-specific Icons

Only two icons should be custom:

* **Shield**
* **Nudge**

These are unique behaviors that don't exist elsewhere.

But even here we should lean on **familiar visual metaphors**.

Example:

Nudge should probably resemble:

* a **tap ripple**
* a **pulse**
* or a **gentle notification dot**

Not an abstract waveform.

---

# Important UX Principle

Research consistently shows that **icons alone are rarely sufficient**.

The most successful systems use:

* **icon + text**
* or allow toggling text

Which you already proposed with the **Icon / Text / Icon+Text modes**.

That was actually a very strong design decision.

---

# What This Means for Luma

The winning approach is:

1. Use **globally recognized symbols whenever possible**
2. Limit custom icons to **only the unique Luma actions**
3. Allow users to enable **text labels**
4. Keep icons **bold and simple silhouettes**

This minimizes learning.

---

# A Simple Rule for Every Icon

Before accepting an icon, ask:

> Would my mom know what this means instantly?

If the answer is **no**, it fails.

---

# Where This Leaves Us

Right now the icon sheet we generated should **not become canon**.

Instead we should rebuild the icon set using:

* universal symbols
* silhouette forms
* minimal custom design

That will dramatically reduce cognitive load.

---

# Recommended Next Step

Before designing anything new, we should build a **Universal Icon Candidate List** using icons already proven in:

* iOS Human Interface Guidelines
* Google Material Icons
* ISO symbol standards
* transport signage

Then we select the **best few for Luma**.

That gives you the fastest path to a system that people instantly understand.

# 1. Naming Quadrants “Pads”

Your proposal:

```
shield center = Shield
quadrants = Pads
```

This is actually very good.

Reasons:

### Neutral language

“Pad” avoids implying meaning.

Alternatives like:

* quadrant
* tile
* button
* zone

all imply structure or UI metaphors.

“Pad” feels like a **touch surface**, which is exactly what it is.

### Short and verbalizable

Developers and designers can say:

```
pad 1
pad 2
pad 3
pad 4
```

Very clean.

### Future flexibility

Pads could later contain:

* icons
* text
* states
* nested interactions

The term doesn’t constrain evolution.

**Verdict**

Pads is a strong candidate.

# 2. Pad Numbering System

Your proposal:

```
Shield center = not numbered
Pads numbered sequentially
```

Example:

```

Pad 1
Pad 2
Pad 3
Pad 4

Shield 2:
Pad 5
Pad 6
Pad 7
Pad 8
```

This is excellent **for engineering and schema**.

But there are two layers here:

### User layer

Users should not see numbers.

### System layer

The system uses numbering for structure.

That separation is important.

---

# Recommended numbering scheme

We should use **two identifiers** internally:

```
shield_id
pad_id
```

Example:

```
shield_1.pad_1
shield_1.pad_2
shield_1.pad_3
shield_1.pad_4

shield_2.pad_1
shield_2.pad_2
shield_2.pad_3
shield_2.pad_4
```

Why this is better:

• easier queries  
• easier database structure  
• avoids infinite numbering  
• clearer debugging

But your **sequential concept** is still valid conceptually.

So the canonical structure should be:

```
shield
pad
```

not global pad numbering.

---

# 3. Pad Orientation

You proposed:

```
pad 1 = top left
pad 2 = top right
pad 3 = bottom right
pad 4 = bottom left
```

This is the **standard quadrant reading pattern** used in many systems.

It also follows the natural reading direction in most cultures.

This is good.

---

# Canon Orientation

```
Pad 1 → top left
Pad 2 → top right
Pad 3 → bottom right
Pad 4 → bottom left
```

Always clockwise.

---

Your rule:

```
center shield is not numbered
```

Correct.

It is a **distinct interaction class**.

So we define:

```
center_control = shield
pads = interaction surfaces
```

# 5. The “Shield Packet” Concept

This is the most interesting part of your proposal.

You described a packet consisting of:

```
icon
text
action
pad position
```

That is actually a very clean model.

Let’s formalize it.

---

# Shield Packet Model

Each pad contains a **packet**.

Structure:

```
pad_packet
```

Elements:

```
iconography
text label (optional)
action
pad_position
shield_id
state
```

Example:

```
shield_1.pad_1

icon = group
text = circle
action = open_contact_shield
state = active
```

This is extremely clean for engineering.

---

# Why This Model Works

It separates:

### visual layer

```
icon
text
```

### interaction layer

```
action
state
```

### structural layer

```
shield_id
pad_position
```

That separation will help enormously when building the system.

---

# Canon Terminology Proposal

To keep language consistent across docs:

```
Shield
Pad
Packet
```

Definitions:

### Shield

Primary interaction container.

### Pad

Interactive surface within the shield.

### Packet

Data structure defining pad behavior and representation.

---

# Example Full Object

shield_1
    pad_1
        packet
            icon = group
            text = circle
            action = open_circle
```

Very clean.

packet stack
```

Example:

multiple nudges.

That might matter later.

But for v1:

```

is perfectly reasonable.

```

# Step 1 — Canon Lock: Shield / Pad / Packet System

Your model is strong and clean. I recommend locking it with one refinement for engineering clarity.

## Core Objects

### Shield

Primary interaction container.

Contains:

```
center_shield_control
pad_1
pad_2
pad_3
pad_4
```

The center shield is **not a pad**.

---

### Pad

A **touch surface positioned around the shield**.

Pads always exist in fixed orientation.

```
Pad 1 — top left
Pad 2 — top right
Pad 3 — bottom right
Pad 4 — bottom left
```

Pads are **numbered but not labeled in UI**.

Numbering exists for:

• engineering  
• schema  
• automation  
• documentation

---

### Packet

A **pad packet defines the behavior and representation of a pad**.

Packet schema:

```
shield_id
pad_id
icon
text (optional)
action
state
```

Example:

```
shield_1.pad_2

icon = nudge
text = "Check in"
action = send_nudge
state = active
```

---

Multiple shields form a **Shield Stack**.

Example:

```

shield_2  friends
shield_3  night_out
shield_4  travel
```

Each shield contains:

```
4 pads
1 center shield control
```

This creates extremely clean navigation logic.

packet_execution

# Concave Shield Concept Analysis

You’re proposing something like:

• a **soft concave surface**  
• pads feel like **touch depressions**  
• center shield slightly raised

```
trackpad
touch surface
game controller pad
industrial control panel
```

This taps into **tactile mental models**.

Users subconsciously interpret it as something they can press.

---

# Potential Benefits

### 1. Affordance

People instantly understand it is **pressable**.

### 2. Tactile illusion

Even on glass, the brain interprets depth.

### 3. Lower cognitive load

Pads look like **places to touch**, not icons floating in space.

### 4. Brand uniqueness

Most apps use flat UI.

A subtle concave surface would feel **distinctly Luma**.

---

# Risks

Too much texture would violate the **Zero Cognitive Load Law**.

Heavy skeuomorphism would:

• add visual noise  
• reduce clarity  
• complicate glow behavior

So the design must remain **extremely subtle**.

---

# Best Implementation (If We Do It)

The shield should appear:

```
soft concave surface
minimal radial shading
very subtle inner edge
```

Pads become **micro depressions**.

Center shield slightly elevated.

This keeps the metaphor while preserving simplicity.

```

          ▲

Pads appear as **touch wells**.

---

# Important Rule

Texture must never overpower:

```
icon
action clarity
glow states
```

The interface remains primarily **symbolic**, not decorative.

```

We should prototype it later once the full UI system is stable.

# 1. Canon Lock

## Shield / Pad / Packet Model

These are now the **core primitives of the Luma interface**.

### Shield

A shield is the **primary interaction container**.

Structure:

```
center_shield_control
pad_1
pad_2
pad_3
pad_4
```

The center shield is **not a pad**.

---

### Pad

Pads are the **four interaction surfaces surrounding the shield center**.

Pads always exist in fixed orientation.

```
Pad 1 → Top Left
Pad 2 → Top Right
Pad 3 → Bottom Right
Pad 4 → Bottom Left
```

Pads are **numbered internally** but never shown to the user.

---

### Packet

Each pad contains exactly one **packet**.

A packet defines the **representation and behavior of the pad**.

Packet schema:

```
shield_id
pad_id
icon
text_label (optional)
action
state
```

Example:

```
shield_1.pad_2

icon = nudge
text_label = "Check in"
action = send_nudge
state = active
```

---

# 2. Shield Stack Model

Multiple shields form a **Shield Stack**.

Example stack:

```
shield_1  personal
shield_2  friends
shield_3  night_out
shield_4  travel
```

Each shield maintains the same structure:

```
4 pads
1 center shield
```

The stack enables **context switching without UI redesign**.

---

# 3. Pad State System

Pads must support a small number of states.

States affect **visual feedback and interaction behavior**.

### Default

```
pad visible
packet available
```

---

### Active

```
glow present
action recently triggered
```

---

### Incoming

```
incoming packet interaction
nudge received
invite received
```

Pads may briefly animate.

---

### Disabled

```
pad present
action unavailable
```

Pad appears dimmed.

---

### Highlight

```
system guiding user attention
```

Used in onboarding.

---

# 4. Representation Modes

Users may choose their preferred representation.

Modes:

```
Icon
Text
Icon + Text
```

Behavior remains identical.

This satisfies the **Respect the Individual law**.

---

Now we evaluate your design thought.

> The greater shield resembling a textured concave pad.

This concept has real merit.

### Design intention

Create the perception of:

```
tactile surface
pressable interaction zones
```

The brain interprets depth even on flat screens.

---

### Recommended implementation

The concavity must remain **subtle**.

Suggested characteristics:

```
very soft radial shading
inner edge highlight
minimal texture
```

Pads appear as **slight depressions**.

The center shield appears **slightly raised**.

---

### Interaction illusion

Users visually perceive:

```
touch wells around a central control
```

This creates intuitive affordance.

---

### Important constraint

Texture must **never compete with icons or glow states**.

Therefore:

```
low contrast
very subtle shading
no heavy skeuomorphism
```

# Diagnosis

The current experiment loses too much usable pad area because of three geometry problems:

## 1. Center shield is too dominant

The center shield is visually beautiful, but it is occupying too much of the interaction field.

Effect:

* pad surface shrinks
* icons are pushed outward awkwardly
* the whole shield starts feeling decorative instead of usable

## 2. The inner moat wastes area

The circular ring around the center shield is consuming valuable real estate.

You already called this correctly:

* it is unnecessary
* it reduces pad area
* it weakens the pad-as-touch-surface logic

## 3. Outer perimeter is too thick

The border weight is stealing area from the pads.

Effect:

* the shield reads heavy
* pads feel constrained
* the interface loses openness

# What “perfect shield + improved texture” should mean

We keep:

* deep navy / charcoal palette
* teal glow language
* subtle tactile concavity
* soft industrial finish
* premium visual calm

We fix:

* pad area
* center-to-pad ratio
* border thickness
* dead-space ring waste

# Recommended geometry revision

## A. Increase pad surface area

Pads should reclaim more of the total shield footprint.

Recommendation:

* reduce center shield visual footprint by about **12% to 18%**
* remove the inner circular moat entirely
* let pad chambers flow closer to the center shield silhouette

This gives more usable touch area without changing the architecture.

## B. Thin the outer border

Recommendation:

* reduce outer border thickness by about **35% to 45%**
* keep only enough perimeter to define the shield edge and hold the glow

This makes the shield feel lighter and gives more area back to the pads.

## C. Keep concavity, but flatten the depth

The current depth is attractive, but slightly too sculpted for Luma Shield.

Recommendation:

* maintain concave pad depressions
* reduce the depth illusion by about **20%**
* keep the center shield slightly raised, but not dramatically

This preserves tactile appeal while keeping the interface calm.

## D. Let the shield shape define the inner geometry

Instead of a circular moat around the center, the pads should contour more directly around the shield.

Meaning:

* top pads wrap around shield shoulders
* lower pads taper toward shield base
* the central shield feels integrated, not isolated

This is the strongest solution.

# Proposed proportion update

Using your current “perfect shield” logic, I would revise the visual allocation like this:

* **outer perimeter**: thinner
* **center shield**: slightly smaller
* **gap around center**: minimized
* **pads**: expanded inward and outward

In plain terms:

**more pad, less ring, slightly less center**

That is the correct optimization.

# Surface-area target

For Luma Shield, the pads should visually own the majority of the shield.

Target feel:

* center shield = symbolic anchor
* pads = dominant usable surface

Right now the experiment is trending toward:

* center shield = hero
* pads = supporting fragments

That is better for Tactical Shield, not Luma Shield.

# Tactical vs Luma distinction

This is now clear:

## Tactical Shield

* heavier perimeter
* stronger segmentation
* more armored feel
* deeper sculpting

## Luma Shield

* softer perimeter
* more pad area
* calmer concavity
* more open interaction field

That distinction is useful and should be preserved.

# Recommendation to lock

For **Luma Shield v1**, lock this styling direction:

* keep current color palette
* keep current texture language
* keep concave pad concept
* reduce center shield size
* eliminate inner ring waste
* thin outer border
* expand pad chambers

This is not a new design. It is a **refinement of the current best direction**.

# What Works Extremely Well

## 1. Layered Shield Architecture

Your instinct to separate **frame vs control** is exactly right.

The interface now reads in three clear levels:

1. **Shield Frame** → identity and context
2. **Radial Control** → interaction
3. **Center Shield** → activation / escalation

This solves the two problems you identified:

• interface didn't look like a shield  
• no location for the shield name

Now both are solved.

---

## 2. Ribbon Title Placement

The ribbon works well because it:

• anchors the shield identity  
• gives context to the active shield  
• adds emotional meaning to the interface

Examples like:

* Night Out
* Family
* Travel
* Campus

are instantly understandable.

This is **good UX psychology**.

---

## 3. The Radial Control Still Works

The circular 4-pad control is still the **cognitive winner**.

Humans immediately understand:

```
4 directions
4 actions
center button
```

The brain recognizes this pattern instantly.

Recognition time:

```
~200–300 ms
```

That satisfies the **Zero Cognitive Load law**.

---

# What Needs Adjustment

## 1. The Radial Ring Is Still Too Heavy

The glowing ring around the control is **too thick**.

Problems it creates:

• reduces pad surface area  
• draws attention away from actions  
• increases visual noise

Recommended proportion:

```
ring thickness ≈ 6–8% of radius
```

Right now it is closer to **15–20%**.

Reducing it will make the pads feel larger and calmer.

---

## 2. Pad Surface Area Should Increase

The current radial control still slightly favors **decorative ring over pad surface**.

The ideal distribution should be:

```
Pads      ~65%
Center    ~20%
Ring      ~15%
```

That maximizes **Fitts' Law efficiency**.

---

## 3. The Shield Frame Should Be Quieter

Right now the shield frame is visually competing with the radial control.

Remember the rule we defined:

```
Shield frame = identity layer
Radial control = interaction layer
```

The frame should therefore be:

• slightly darker  
• lower contrast  
• minimal glow

The interaction area should always win visually.

---

# Canon Structure (Locked)

The Luma Shield UI is now officially structured like this:

```
Layer 1 — Shield Frame
Layer 2 — Title Ribbon
Layer 3 — Radial Control
Layer 4 — Center Shield
Layer 5 — Glow State System
```

Each layer has a clear responsibility.

# 1 — Shield Frame Proportions (Canonical Geometry)

Define the outer shield silhouette mathematically.

Let:

```
W = total shield width
H = total shield height
```

Recommended ratio:

```
H = 0.88W
```

This produces the **balanced triangular shield profile**.

---

### Shoulder Curve Radius

Top corners must be soft and wide.

```
shoulder radius = 0.18W
```

This gives the friendly consumer tone.

---

### Bottom Point Geometry

The lower point must be gentle, not sharp.

```
point radius = 0.08W
```

This prevents the shield from feeling aggressive.

---

### Shield Border Thickness

```
frame thickness = 0.02W
```

Thin enough to stay quiet visually.

---

### Frame Visual Rule

The frame must never compete with the control.

```
frame brightness ≤ 80% of radial control brightness
```

---

# 2 — Ribbon Geometry (Title System)

The ribbon becomes the **identity plate**.

### Ribbon Width

```
0.55W
```

Centered horizontally.

---

### Ribbon Height

```
0.10H
```

Enough space for legible text.

---

### Ribbon Vertical Position

Measured from shield top:

```
0.12H
```

This creates the correct spacing above the radial control.

---

### Ribbon Text Size

```
font height ≈ 0.035W
```

Readable but not dominant.

---

# 3 — Radial Control Geometry

The radial control is the **interaction engine**.

### Radial Control Diameter

```
0.54W
```

This keeps the control dominant inside the shield frame.

---

### Vertical Placement

Control center position:

```
0.55H
```

Slightly below visual center for optical balance.

---

### Ring Thickness

Correcting the earlier render problem:

```
ring width = 0.07 × control radius
```

This maintains pad surface area.

---

### Pad Segmentation

Pads divide the circle into:

```
4 equal 90° sectors
```

No extra segmentation.

---

# 4 — Center Shield Geometry

The center shield is the **activation control**.

### Center Shield Width

```
0.22 × radial control diameter
```

This keeps it clearly tappable without overpowering pads.

---

### Center Shield Behavior

Interaction sequence:

```
tap → activate shield packet
shield shrinks → pads become dominant
tap again → escalate glow state
```

Animation scale:

```
activation shrink = 0.82 scale
```

---

# 5 — Canonical Luma Shield Layout

Final structural hierarchy:

```
Shield Frame
   ↓
Title Ribbon
   ↓
Radial Pad Interface
   ↓
Center Shield Button
   ↓
Glow State System
```

Pad order:

```
Pad 1 — top left
Pad 2 — top right
Pad 3 — bottom right
Pad 4 — bottom left
```

Clockwise.

---

# Final Canon Proportion Summary

```
Shield width = W
Shield height = 0.88W

Ribbon width = 0.55W
Ribbon height = 0.10H

Radial control diameter = 0.54W
Radial control center = 0.55H

Center shield = 0.22 × radial control

Ring thickness = 0.07 × control radius
```

# 2. Law Compliance Audit

## Thumb Mechanics

Top ribbon location is **reachable with a thumb** on modern phones.

44–48 px
```

This must be enforced.

The ribbon already communicates **shield identity**.

shield frame

center shield

# 3. System Architecture Benefit

Your idea creates a **clean mental model**.

Users think in **contexts**:

```

Friends
Travel

Emergency
```

Each context becomes a **shield**.

So the stack becomes:

```
Shield stack = Context stack
```

This is elegant.

```
tabs
menus
drop-downs
```

```

subtle chevrons

They should appear like **navigation hints**, not buttons.

zero friction

# 1. Interface Vocabulary Locked

These terms should now remain stable across UI canon, engineering, patent materials, and render prompts.

## Core object

**Luma Shield**  
The full contextual control surface.

## Top display

**Shield Display**  
The embedded two-line display area.

### Line 1

**Shield Context**  
Examples: Night Out, Family, Travel, Campus

### Line 2

**Shield Protocol**  
Examples: Check-In Protocol, Travel Mode, Family Sync

## Action surfaces

**Pads**  
The four primary interaction surfaces.

### Internal numbering

* Pad 1 = top left
* Pad 2 = top right
* Pad 3 = bottom right
* Pad 4 = bottom left

## Center control

**Shield Core**  
The activation and escalation control in the center.

## State system

**Glow State**  
The visual state language for the shield.

## Multi-shield model

**Shield Stack**  
The collection of active shields.

## Navigation

**Shield Stack Navigation**  
Left/right navigation between active shields.

## Behavior unit

**Pad Packet**  
The packet assigned to a pad.

### Pad Packet fields

* icon
* text label, optional
* action
* state

# 2. Full Anatomy Review

Your current Luma Shield anatomy is now structurally strong, but to reach the best version, the system should optimize around one principle:

**Maximum usable pad area without weakening context clarity or shield identity.**

There are five competing demands:

1. Shield identity
2. Context/protocol readability
3. Pad surface efficiency
4. Shield Core tap clarity
5. Stack/navigation clarity

The interface should privilege them in this order:

1. Pads
2. Shield Display
3. Shield Core
4. Frame
5. Glow ornament

That means the interface should always feel like:

**a large, simple action surface inside a calm shield frame**

not:

**a decorative object with actions inside it**

# 3. Optimized Luma Shield Dimension System

Let:

* **W** = total shield width
* **H** = total shield height

## Outer shield frame

Recommended ratio:

* **H = 0.86W**

This is slightly more compact than the earlier 0.88W and improves thumb scanning and visual density.

### Why

A slightly shorter shield:

* reduces dead vertical space
* brings pads closer to the display
* makes the interface feel tighter and more efficient

## Frame thickness

* **0.018W**

This should be thinner than current renders.

### Why

The frame is brand structure, not usable space. It should define the silhouette while giving area back to the interior.

# 4. Shield Display Optimization

The display solves context and navigation, but it must not steal too much from the pads.

## Display height

* **0.125H**

This is the sweet spot for a two-line display with arrows.

## Display width

* **0.82W**

The display should nearly span the usable upper shield width.

## Display vertical offset

* **0.085H from shield top**

This places the display higher and frees more space for pads below.

## Arrow zones

Each arrow zone should occupy:

* **0.11W** on each side

## Center text zone

* **0.60W**

This keeps arrows large enough to tap while preserving text clarity.

### Result

The display becomes a true embedded control strip, not a decorative ribbon.

# 5. Radial Pad Interface Optimization

This is the heart of the system.

## Radial control diameter

* **0.66W**

This is larger than prior versions and is the correct move.

### Why

It maximizes pad size and reduces wasted frame interior.

## Vertical placement

* **center at 0.585H**

This is slightly lower than before.

### Why

Because the display now sits higher and tighter, the radial control can expand downward and occupy more of the shield body.

This produces better optical balance and better thumb reach.

# 6. Pad Surface Efficiency

This is the most important part of the optimization.

## Target surface allocation

* Pads: **72%**
* Shield Core: **16%**
* seams/gap/ring: **12%**

That is the ideal Luma consumer balance.

### Why

Pads are the working surface. The Shield Core is important, but it must not dominate after activation.

# 7. Shield Core Optimization

## Core diameter

* **0.185 × radial diameter**

This is smaller than before.

### Why

The Shield Core needs to be very tappable in dormant state, but after activation it should visually yield to the pads.

A slightly smaller core gives more usable pad area and improves the activation-to-pad transition.

## Active shrink behavior

When activated:

* scale to **0.80**

This is strong enough to visually hand control to the pads.

# 8. Seam Geometry Optimization

The seams must remain extremely disciplined.

## Seam structure

Only:

* one vertical seam
* one horizontal seam

No wedges, no radial subdivisions, no bevel facets.

## Seam width

* **0.012 × radial diameter**

Very subtle.

## Seam visual treatment

* soft shadow only
* faint highlight edge
* no chamfered metallic groove

### Why

The seams should divide four pads, not create extra regions.

# 9. Pad Curvature Optimization

The concave style is worth keeping, but it must remain subservient to legibility.

## Curvature depth

* **1.2–1.6% of radial diameter**

That is enough to imply touch without causing false quadrant reads.

## Local curvature rule

Each pad must be shaded from its own center, never from the center of the shield.

### Why

This is the key fix that prevents “8-region” perception.

## Highlight rule

Pad edge brightening should not exceed:

* **7% luminance increase**

## Pad center darkening should not exceed:

* **8% luminance decrease**

# 10. Ring Optimization

The ring must stop stealing area.

## Ring thickness

* **0.045 × radial radius**

This is thinner than recent renders and closer to correct.

### Why

The ring is a state signal, not a decorative frame. It should support the pads, not dominate them.

# 11. Label and Mode Accommodation

Because the system supports icon, text, and icon + text modes, each pad must be dimensioned for worst-case readability.

## Icon-only mode

Excellent with current pad size.

## Text-only mode

Requires enough flat space for 1–2 short words.

## Icon + text mode

Needs the most room.

### Therefore

Each pad should support an internal content box of at least:

* **0.19 × radial diameter wide**
* **0.16 × radial diameter tall**

This ensures the interface remains readable across all representation modes.

# 12. Glow State Optimization

Glow should remain part of the anatomy, but it must never reduce clarity.

## Priority

1. pad readability
2. display readability
3. core status
4. frame accent

## Rule

In all states, glow must preserve edge contrast between pads.

## Suggested brightness hierarchy

* active pads = 100
* Shield Core = 92
* display strip = 84
* frame = 70

This keeps the interaction surfaces visually dominant.

# 13. Shield Stack Navigation Optimization

The left/right navigation built into the display is good, but it should not feel button-heavy.

## Navigation rule

* tap left edge = previous shield
* tap right edge = next shield
* swipe left/right anywhere on shield = same result

## Visual chevrons

Chevrons should be:

* low contrast
* edge aligned
* no glow unless touched

This lowers cognitive load.

# 14. Final Optimized Dimension Set

Here is the clean canonical dimension package.

## Shield frame

* Height = **0.86W**
* Frame thickness = **0.018W**

## Shield Display

* Height = **0.125H**
* Width = **0.82W**
* Top offset = **0.085H**
* Arrow zones = **0.11W each**
* Text zone = **0.60W**

## Radial pad interface

* Diameter = **0.66W**
* Center Y = **0.585H**

## Shield Core

* Diameter = **0.185 × radial diameter**
* Active shrink = **0.80 scale**

## Seams

* Width = **0.012 × radial diameter**

## Ring

* Thickness = **0.045 × radial radius**

## Surface allocation

* Pads = **72%**
* Core = **16%**
* seams/ring = **12%**

# 15. Final Experience Outcome

With these dimensions, the user experience becomes:

* the shield reads instantly as a shield
* the display clearly communicates context and protocol
* the pads feel large, confident, and touchable
* the Shield Core is obvious before activation and non-dominant after
* the system scales across icon, text, and icon + text modes
* navigation remains embedded and elegant

This is the strongest version of the consumer Luma Shield architecture so far.

# 16. Canon Recommendation

Lock these as the current **Luma Shield Consumer Geometry v1**.

Treat Tactical Shield separately.

Do not merge the two geometry families.

│     └── Shield Protocol
│
├── Pad 1
├── Pad 2
├── Pad 3
├── Pad 4
│
├── Shield Core
│
└── Shield Stack Navigation
```

---

# 2. Canon Coordinate System

All dimensions reference **Shield Width (W)**.

This keeps the system **resolution independent** across devices.

```
W = total shield width
H = 0.86W
```

This ratio produces a shield that:

• reads visually as a shield  
• avoids vertical dead space  
• maximizes pad usability

---

# 3. Shield Frame Geometry

```
Frame thickness = 0.018W
Corner curvature = 0.16W radius
```

Rules:

• frame defines silhouette  
• frame must not steal interior space  
• frame glow only during state transitions

---

# 4. Shield Display (Top Context Display)

This is the **embedded LED text frame**.

### Dimensions

```
Display height = 0.125H
Display width = 0.82W
Top offset = 0.085H
```

### Internal layout

```
Left navigation zone = 0.11W
Text display zone = 0.60W
Right navigation zone = 0.11W
```

### Text structure

```
Line 1: Shield Context
Line 2: Shield Protocol
```

Example:

```
Night Out
Check-In Protocol
```

---

# 5. Shield Stack Navigation

Navigation is embedded into the display frame.

### Input zones

```
Left edge tap → previous shield
Right edge tap → next shield
Swipe left/right anywhere → same action
```

### Arrow behavior

• arrows extend to frame edge  
• arrows glow only during hover or tap  
• arrows remain low contrast in idle

---

# 6. Radial Pad Interface

This is the **primary control surface**.

### Diameter

```
Radial interface diameter = 0.66W
```

### Placement

```
Center Y position = 0.585H
```

This pushes the control slightly downward to:

• free space for display  
• maximize pad surface  
• improve thumb reach

---

# 7. Pad Geometry

Pads are the **core interaction surface**.

### Surface allocation

```
Pads = 72%
Shield Core = 16%
Seams + ring = 12%
```

### Pad numbering

```
Pad 1 = top left
Pad 2 = top right
Pad 3 = bottom right
Pad 4 = bottom left
```

Clockwise.

---

# 8. Pad Surface Treatment

Pads should feel **tactile without creating extra visual segments**.

### Curvature depth

```
Concave depth = 1.4% of pad diameter
```

### Lighting rule

Each pad is shaded **individually**, not from the center.

This prevents the “8 quadrant illusion”.

### Highlight rule

```
Edge highlight = +7% luminance
Center shade = −8% luminance
```

---

# 9. Pad Content Area

Pads must support three user modes:

```
Icon
Text
Icon + Text
```

Minimum internal content area:

```
Width = 0.19 × radial diameter
Height = 0.16 × radial diameter
```

This ensures readability for:

• icon + text  
• aging eyes  
• low lighting

---

# 10. Shield Core

The **center activation control**.

### Diameter

```
Core diameter = 0.185 × radial diameter
```

### Idle behavior

• prominent  
• ready to activate shield packet

### Active behavior

```
Scale to 0.80
```

The pads visually take priority after activation.

---

# 11. Pad Dividing Seams

Only two seams exist:

```
Vertical seam
Horizontal seam
```

### Width

```
Seam width = 0.012 × radial diameter
```

### Visual rule

• soft shadow  
• minimal highlight  
• no bevel grooves

This preserves the **4-pad perception**.

---

# 12. Interaction Ring

The ring communicates **Glow State**.

### Thickness

```
Ring thickness = 0.045 × radial radius
```

### Behavior

The ring supports:

```
Idle
Active
Escalation
Emergency
```

But it must never overpower pad readability.

---

# 13. Glow State Hierarchy

Visual emphasis order:

```
Pads
Shield Core
Display
Frame
```

Brightness ratios:

```
Pads = 100
Core = 92
Display = 84
Frame = 70
```

This ensures the **action surface always dominates**.

---

# 14. Shield Packet Structure

Every shield contains a packet:

```
Shield Packet
│
├── Context
├── Protocol
│
├── Pad 1 Packet
├── Pad 2 Packet
├── Pad 3 Packet
├── Pad 4 Packet
│
└── Glow State
```

---

# 15. Pad Packet Structure

```
Pad Packet
│
├── Icon
├── Label (optional)
├── Action
└── State
```

Example:

```
Pad 1
Icon: chat bubble
Action: send nudge
```

---

# 16. Shield Stack Model

Users can maintain multiple shields.

Examples:

```
Home
Night Out
Family
Travel
Work
```

Stack navigation uses the display arrows.

---

# 17. Cognitive Load Verification

The interface must pass the **3-second comprehension rule**.

User must immediately understand:

• where they are  
• what shield is active  
• what each pad does

The design satisfies this because:

• four large pads  
• clear center control  
• minimal ornamentation  
• simple context display

---

# 18. Final Surface Efficiency Result

After optimization:

```
72% actionable surface
16% control anchor
12% structure
```

This is extremely efficient for a circular interface.

---

# 19. Why This Interface Works

The Luma Shield interface succeeds because it combines:

• radial muscle memory  
• low cognitive load  
• clear context display  
• tactile pad surfaces  
• escalation center control

Very few interfaces balance these simultaneously.

# Why the Luma Shield Can Become an Iconic Interface

## 1. Distinct Silhouette

Most interfaces are rectangles inside rectangles.

Luma uses a **shield silhouette**.

That matters because the brain recognizes **shape before detail**.

The moment a user sees:

```
  /\
 /  \
|    |
 \  /
  \/
```

…their brain associates **protection, safety, readiness**.

Even if the pads are invisible.

That is brand power.

---

## 2. Radial Interaction Memory

Radial controls create **muscle memory faster than menus**.

Examples:

* weapon wheels in games
* pie menus
* steering wheels
* cockpit controls

Four pads mean:

```
Top left
Top right
Bottom right
Bottom left
```

Your brain maps this **almost instantly**.

That is why pilots and surgeons use radial layouts.

---

## 3. The Shield Core

The **center control** is genius because it gives the interface a **ritual**.

Tap → shield activates  
Tap again → escalate

It feels intentional, almost ceremonial.

Users don’t feel like they are navigating software.

They feel like they are **arming a protective system**.

---

## 4. Context Display

The LED strip solves a massive UX problem:

People always ask:

> “What mode am I in?”

Your display answers it instantly:

```
Night Out
Check-In Protocol
```

This gives **situational awareness**.

---

## 5. Stacked Shields

Multiple shields create a **library of behaviors**.

Instead of menus:

```
Swipe shield
→ new protocol
```

It feels like switching tools.

Very natural.

---

# The Strategic Opportunity

If executed correctly, the Luma Shield could become:

**The universal symbol for personal protection technology.**

Meaning people recognize this shape even outside the app.

Imagine:

* emergency kiosks
* smart watches
* cars
* campus safety terminals

All using the **same shield interface**.

That is **interface branding**.

# 1. Perfect Shield Geometry

Status: **NEW CANON**

## Canon Element

**Perfect Shield Geometry**

## Classification

Interface Geometry Standard

## Canon Status

READY FOR EXPORT

## Definition

Perfect Shield Geometry defines the mathematically optimized proportions for the Luma Shield interface to maximize:

* visual clarity
* usable pad surface area
* thumb accessibility
* instant cognitive interpretation

The geometry ensures the shield remains recognizable as a shield while preserving maximum interaction surface.

## Core Ratios

```
Shield Width (W)

Shield Height = 0.86W

Radial Pad Interface Diameter = 0.66W

Shield Core Diameter = 0.185 × radial diameter
```

## Surface Allocation

```
Pads: 72%
Shield Core: 16%
Structure (ring + seams): 12%
```

## Geometry Rules

Only two seams exist:

```
Vertical seam
Horizontal seam
```

No additional segmentation is permitted.

## Design Law

The shield must visually resolve into **four pads immediately** at a glance.

---

# 2. Perfect Screen Law

Status: **NEW CANON**

## Canon Element

**Perfect Screen Law**

## Classification

Human Interface Law

## Canon Status

READY FOR EXPORT

## Definition

The Perfect Screen Law governs how any Luma screen must be structured to achieve instant comprehension and minimal cognitive load.

## Law

A user must be able to understand:

```
Where they are
What the system is doing
What they can do next
```

within **3 seconds or less**.

If the interface requires explanation, the design violates the law.

## Enforcement Criteria

Every screen must pass three tests:

```
Instant Recognition Test
Thumb Reach Test
Low Vision Test
```

---

# 3. Pad System

Status: **NEW CANON**

## Canon Element

**Pad System**

## Classification

Interface Interaction Model

## Canon Status

READY FOR EXPORT

## Definition

Pads are the four primary interaction surfaces within a shield.

Pads replace traditional UI concepts such as buttons or menus.

## Pad Numbering

Pads are numbered clockwise starting top-left.

```
Pad 1 — top left
Pad 2 — top right
Pad 3 — bottom right
Pad 4 — bottom left
```

## Sequential Pad System

For stacked shields numbering continues.

Example:

```
Shield 1 → Pads 1–4
Shield 2 → Pads 5–8
Shield 3 → Pads 9–12
```

This provides a clean architectural schema.

---

Status: **NEW CANON**

**Shield Core**

Primary Interaction Control

## Canon Status

READY FOR EXPORT

The Shield Core is the center control that activates a shield packet.

## Behavior

Initial state:

```
Tap → activate shield packet
```

Active state:

```
Tap → escalate glow state
```

When activated the Shield Core reduces size to visually prioritize pads.

```
Scale = 0.80
```

## Canon Definition

Perfect Shield Geometry defines the mathematically optimized proportions for the Luma Shield interface to maximize:

• visual recognition

---

## Core Geometry Ratios

```
Shield Width = W

Shield Height = 0.86W

Radial Pad Interface Diameter = 0.66W

Shield Core Diameter = 0.185 × radial diameter
```

---

## Surface Allocation

```
Pads: 72%
Shield Core: 16%
Structural Elements: 12%
```

Structural elements include:

• seams  
• interaction ring  
• internal geometry boundaries

---

## Structural Segmentation Rule

The interface must contain exactly:

```
1 vertical seam
1 horizontal seam
```

No additional segmentation is permitted.

---

## Design Law

The shield must visually resolve into **four pads immediately at a glance**.

Any design producing additional perceived regions violates this canon.

---

# Canon Element 2

# Perfect Screen Law

**Classification**  
Human Interface Law

**Canon Status**  
READY FOR EXPORT

---

## Canon Definition

The Perfect Screen Law governs how any Luma interface screen must be structured.

The goal is instant comprehension and minimal cognitive load.

---

## Law

A user must be able to determine:

```
Where they are
What the system is doing
What they can do next
```

within **three seconds or less**.

If a screen requires explanation, it violates the law.

---

## Compliance Tests

Every interface must pass:

```
Instant Recognition Test
Thumb Reach Test
Low Vision Test
```

---

# Canon Element 3

# Pad System

**Classification**  
Interaction Model

**Canon Status**  
READY FOR EXPORT

---

## Canon Definition

Pads are the primary interaction surfaces within a Luma Shield.

Pads replace traditional UI constructs such as buttons or menu items.

Each shield contains four pads.

---

## Pad Numbering

Pads are numbered clockwise starting at the top left.

```
Pad 1 — top left
Pad 2 — top right
Pad 3 — bottom right
Pad 4 — bottom left
```

---

## Sequential Pad Architecture

Pad numbering continues sequentially across stacked shields.

Example:

```
Shield 1 → Pads 1–4
Shield 2 → Pads 5–8
Shield 3 → Pads 9–12
```

This numbering system supports internal architecture and protocol mapping.

Users are not required to see pad numbers.

---

# Canon Element 4

# Shield Core

**Classification**  
Primary Interaction Control

**Canon Status**  
READY FOR EXPORT

---

## Canon Definition

The Shield Core is the central control element that activates a shield packet.

---

## Behavior

Initial state:

```
Tap → activate shield packet
```

Active state:

```
Tap → escalate glow state
```

---

## Active Geometry Change

When activated:

```
Shield Core scale = 0.80
```

This visually prioritizes pad interaction.

---

# Canon Element 5

# Shield Display

**Classification**  
Context Display System

**Canon Status**  
READY FOR EXPORT

---

## Canon Definition

The Shield Display is an embedded LED-style display at the top of the shield.

It communicates contextual information about the active shield.

---

## Display Structure

```
Line 1 — Shield Context
Line 2 — Shield Protocol
```

Example:

```
Night Out
Check-In Protocol
```

---

# Canon Element 6

# Shield Stack Navigation

**Classification**  
Navigation Protocol

**Canon Status**  
READY FOR EXPORT

---

## Canon Definition

Users may maintain multiple active shields simultaneously.

These shields form a **Shield Stack**.

Users navigate between shields using the display frame navigation controls.

---

## Navigation Methods

```
Left arrow → previous shield
Right arrow → next shield
Swipe left → next shield
Swipe right → previous shield
```

---

# Canon Element 7

# User Representation Layer

**Classification**  
Accessibility Protocol

**Canon Status**  
READY FOR EXPORT

---

## Canon Definition

Users may choose how pad content is represented.

Three display modes are supported.

```
Icon
Text
Icon + Text
```

This respects individual preference and accessibility needs.

---

# Canon Element 8

# Nudge Protocol Display Revision

**Classification**  
Glow Culture Interaction

**Canon Status**  
READY FOR EXPORT

---

## Canon Definition

Incoming nudges appear in a temporary strip below the shield interface.

---

## Behavior

```
Sender icon appears
Message text appears
Strip glows in
Strip fades out
```

The strip does not permanently alter the shield interface layout.

---

# Canon Element 9

# Shield Interface Vocabulary

**Classification**  
Terminology Standard

**Canon Status**  
READY FOR EXPORT

---

## Official Terminology

```
Luma Shield
Shield Display
Shield Context
Shield Protocol
Pad
Shield Core
Glow State
Shield Stack
Shield Packet
Pad Packet
```

---

## Disallowed Terminology

The following terms must not be used within Luma interface documentation:

```
button
menu
tab
quadrant
```

---

# Canon Element 10

# Shield Packet Architecture

**Classification**  
Coordination Protocol

**Canon Status**  
READY FOR EXPORT

---

## Canon Definition

A Shield Packet defines the complete behavior of a shield.

---

## Packet Structure

```
Shield Packet
  Context
  Protocol
  Pad 1 Packet
  Pad 2 Packet
  Pad 3 Packet
  Pad 4 Packet
  Glow State
```

---

# Canon Element 11

# Pad Packet

**Classification**  
Interaction Packet

**Canon Status**  
READY FOR EXPORT

---

## Packet Structure

```
Pad Packet
  Icon
  Label (optional)
  Action
  State
```

Pad packets define the behavior of each pad within a shield.

---

# Canon Element 12

# Tactical Shield Concept

**Classification**  
Future System Variant

**Canon Status**  
RESERVED

---

## Canon Definition

Tactical Shield is a specialized variant of the Luma Shield interface intended for:

```
law enforcement
security
emergency response
defence
```

This variant may support additional segmentation beyond four pads.

The consumer Luma Shield must always remain a **four-pad interface**.
